- Official syllabus 2025 -
- Consolidated data - All important definitions -
Concept Source Formal Definition
1. ETHICS & MORAL PHILOSOPHY
Ethics Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The philosophical study of morality.”
Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Moral principles that govern a person's behaviour or the conducting of an activity.”
Morality Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The standards that an individual or a group has about what is right and wrong or good and evil.”
Meta-Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The attempt to understand the metaphysical, epistemological, semantic, and psychological presuppositions and commitments of moral thought, talk, and practice.”
Normative Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of ethical action. It is the branch of ethics that investigates the set of questions that arise when considering how one ought to act, morally speaking.”
Applied Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The application of normative ethical theories to practical moral problems.”
Descriptive Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The scientific study of moral beliefs and practices.”
Ethical Dilemma Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “A situation in which a difficult choice has to be made between two courses of action, each of which entails violating a moral requirement.”
Moral Agent Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A being who is capable of acting with reference to right and wrong.”
Moral Patient Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A being that can be wronged, even if it cannot wrong others.”
Moral Realism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that there are objective moral facts.”
Moral Anti-Realism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The denial of moral realism.”
Moral Absolutism Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The view that certain actions are absolutely right or wrong, regardless of context.”
Moral Relativism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that moral judgments are true or false only relative to some particular standpoint (for example, that of a culture or a historical period) and that no standpoint is uniquely privileged.”
Cultural Relativism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The thesis that a person’s moral code is determined by the society or culture in which they live.”
Moral Universalism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The metaethical position that some system of ethics, or a universal ethic, applies universally, that is, for ‘all similarly situated individuals’, regardless of culture, race, sex, religion, nationality, sexual orientation, or any other distinguishing feature.”
Ethical Naturalism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that moral terms and properties can be defined in naturalistic terms.”
Ethical Non-Naturalism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that moral properties are not natural properties and cannot be defined in naturalistic terms.”
Emotivism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The metaethical view that moral judgments express emotions or attitudes, rather than propositions that can be true or false.”
Prescriptivism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that moral judgments are prescriptions or imperatives.”
Error Theory Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that all moral statements are false.”
Moral Nihilism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that there are no moral facts or moral truths.”
Moral Skepticism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that we cannot have moral knowledge.”
Amoral Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Lacking a moral sense; unconcerned with the rightness or wrongness of something.”
Immoral Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Not conforming to accepted standards of morality.”
Non-Moral Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “Not involving questions of right or wrong.”
Moral Epistemology Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of moral knowledge.”
Moral Ontology Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of the existence and nature of moral facts or properties.”
Moral Motivation Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The psychological process by which moral judgments lead to action.”
Internalism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that there is a necessary connection between moral judgment and motivation.”
Externalism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that moral judgment and motivation can come apart.”
Moral Luck Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The phenomenon whereby a moral agent can be appropriately praised or blamed for an action even though a significant aspect of what they did depended on factors beyond their control.”
Supererogation Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The performance of more than is required by duty.”
Moral Saint Susan Wolf, "Moral Saints" (1982) “A person who is as morally good as can be.”
Moral Perfectionism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that moral agents should aim for moral perfection.”
Moral Status Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The degree to which an entity is morally considerable.”
Personhood Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The status of being a person, typically associated with certain rights and duties.”
Sentience APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The capacity to have feelings, especially the capacity to experience pleasure and pain.”
Moral Community Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The group of beings to whom moral obligations are owed.”
Moral Circle Peter Singer, "The Expanding Circle" (1981) “The range of beings whose interests ought to be considered from a moral point of view.”
Moral Progress Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Improvement in moral beliefs or practices over time.”
Moral Regress Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Deterioration in moral beliefs or practices over time.”
Moral Enhancement Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The use of biomedical or other means to improve moral capacities.”
Moral Bioenhancement Ingmar Persson & Julian Savulescu, "Unfit for the Future" (2012) “The use of biomedical interventions to improve moral motivation or moral cognition.”
Moral Injury APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Psychological distress caused by actions (or the inability to prevent actions) that violate one’s moral or ethical code.”
Moral Distress APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The anguish experienced when one is unable to act according to one’s moral beliefs due to external constraints.”
Moral Panic Oxford Dictionary of Sociology (2020) “A widespread feeling of fear or concern that some evil threatens the well-being of society.”
Moral Entrepreneurship Howard Becker, "Outsiders" (1963) “Individuals who create or enforce moral rules.”
Moral Imperialism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The imposition of one society’s moral values on another.”
Moral Cosmopolitanism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that all human beings belong to a single moral community.”
2. MAJOR ETHICAL THEORIES
Virtue Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The ethical theory that emphasizes the virtues, or moral character, in contrast to the approach which emphasizes duties or rules (deontology) or that which emphasizes the consequences of actions (consequentialism).”
Deontology Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The normative ethical position that judges the morality of an action based on rules or duties.”
Consequentialism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that the rightness or wrongness of actions depends only on their consequences.”
Utilitarianism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The ethical theory that the right action is the one that produces the greatest balance of good over evil.”
Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The ethical doctrine that the right action is that which produces the greatest happiness of the greatest number.”
Act Utilitarianism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that the rightness of an individual action is determined directly by whether it maximizes utility.”
Rule Utilitarianism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that an action is right if it conforms to a rule that, if generally followed, would maximize utility.”
Preference Utilitarianism Peter Singer, "Practical Ethics" (2011) “The theory that the best action is the one that satisfies the most preferences.”
Hedonistic Utilitarianism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The version of utilitarianism that identifies well-being with pleasure and the absence of pain.”
Care Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A normative ethical theory that holds that moral action centers on interpersonal relationships and care or benevolence as a virtue.”
Rights-Based Ethics Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “Any ethical theory that gives priority to the protection of individual rights.”
Social Contract Theory Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that morality is founded on an agreement among rational individuals.”
Egoism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The normative position that moral agents ought to act in their own self-interest.”
Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The doctrine that self-interest is the foundation of morality.”
Ethical Egoism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The normative theory that one ought to promote one’s own good.”
Psychological Egoism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The descriptive thesis that all voluntary actions are motivated by self-interest.”
Altruism APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Behavior that is motivated by concern for others or by internalized values, goals, and self-rewards rather than by the expectation of concrete, external rewards.”
Effective Altruism Peter Singer, "The Life You Can Save" (2009) “A philosophy and social movement that applies evidence and reason to working out the most effective ways to improve the world.”
Divine Command Theory Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that morality is dependent upon God, and that moral obligation consists in obedience to God’s commands.”
Natural Law Theory Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The theory that an eternal, absolute moral law can be discovered by reason in human nature and/or the nature of the universe.”
Particularism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that there are no moral principles and that moral judgment can only be made on a case-by-case basis.”
Situationism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The thesis that human behavior is more influenced by situational factors than by stable character traits.”
Contractarianism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that morality is based on a social contract agreed to by rational self-interested agents.”
Contractualism T.M. Scanlon, "What We Owe to Each Other" (1998) “An action is wrong if its performance under the circumstances would be disallowed by any set of principles for the general regulation of behavior that no one could reasonably reject as a basis for informed, unforced, general agreement.”
Intuitionism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that we have intuitive knowledge of moral truths.”
Sentimentalism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that moral judgments are based on sentiments or emotions.”
Rossian Deontology W.D. Ross, "The Right and the Good" (1930) “The theory that we have a number of prima facie duties which must be weighed against each other in particular situations.”
Prima Facie Duty W.D. Ross, "The Right and the Good" (1930) “A duty that is binding unless overridden by a stronger duty.”
Kantian Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The ethical theory developed by Immanuel Kant, based on the idea that rational beings should act according to universal moral laws.”
Categorical Imperative Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “Act only according to that maxim whereby you can at the same time will that it should become a universal law.”
Hypothetical Imperative Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “If you will X, then do Y.”
Kingdom of Ends Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “A systematic union of rational beings under common moral laws.”
Autonomy Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “The property of the will by which it is a law to itself.”
Heteronomy Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “The will being determined by something other than itself.”
Maxim Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “A subjective principle of volition.”
Universal Law Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “A law that holds for all rational beings.”
Humanity Formula Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “Act in such a way that you treat humanity, whether in your own person or in the person of any other, never merely as a means to an end, but always at the same time as an end.”
Dignity Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “An unconditional, incomparable worth that admits of no equivalent.”
Respect for Persons Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “The attitude of recognizing the dignity of rational beings.”
Good Will Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “The only thing that is good without qualification.”
Duty Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “The necessity of acting from respect for the moral law.”
Perfect Duty Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “A duty that admits of no exception in favor of inclination.”
Imperfect Duty Kant, "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" (1785) “A duty that allows for latitude in its fulfillment.”
Consequentialist Teleology Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that the right action is the one that promotes the best state of affairs.”
Deontological Constraint Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A moral restriction that prohibits certain types of action even if they would produce better consequences.”
Agent-Centered Prerogative Samuel Scheffler, "The Rejection of Consequentialism" (1982) “A permission for agents to give disproportionate weight to their own interests.”
Threshold Deontology Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that deontological constraints hold up to a certain threshold, beyond which consequentialist reasons can override them.”
Scalar Consequentialism Alastair Norcross, "Reasons Without Demands" (2006) “The view that actions are better or worse depending on how much good they produce, but there is no threshold for moral obligation.”
Satisficing Consequentialism Michael Slote, "Common-Sense Morality and Consequentialism" (1984) “The view that an action is permissible if it produces a good enough outcome, even if not the best possible.”
Negative Consequentialism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that we are morally required to minimize harm but not necessarily to maximize good.”
Population Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of the ethical implications of policies that affect the number, identity, and welfare of future people.”
Repugnant Conclusion Derek Parfit, "Reasons and Persons" (1984) “For any possible population of at least ten billion people, all with a very high quality of life, there must be some much larger imaginable population whose existence, if other things are equal, would be better, even though its members have lives that are barely worth living.”
Non-Identity Problem Derek Parfit, "Reasons and Persons" (1984) “The puzzle that actions which affect who will exist in the future may not harm or benefit anyone, because the people who would have existed otherwise never exist.”
Person-Affecting View Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that an outcome can only be better or worse if it is better or worse for someone.”
Total View Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that the value of a population depends on the total amount of welfare it contains.”
Average View Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that the value of a population depends on the average welfare of its members.”
Prioritarianism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that benefiting people matters more the worse off they are.”
Egalitarianism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that equality has intrinsic moral value.”
Sufficientarianism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that what matters is that everyone has enough.”
Luck Egalitarianism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that inequalities due to luck are unjust, but inequalities due to choice are permissible.”
Relational Egalitarianism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that justice requires equal social relations, not merely equal distribution.”
Capability Approach Amartya Sen, "Commodities and Capabilities" (1999) “A theoretical framework that entails two core normative claims: first, the claim that the freedom to achieve well-being is of primary moral importance, and second, that freedom to achieve well-being is to be understood in terms of people’s capabilities.”
3. KEY CONCEPTS IN ETHICS
Justice Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The constant and perpetual disposition to render every man his due.”
Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The moral virtue that consists in giving each person what she is owed.”
Fairness Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Impartial and just treatment or behaviour without favouritism or discrimination.”
Rights Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Justified claims that individuals or groups can make upon others or upon society.”
Liberty Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The condition of being free from restriction or control by others.”
Equality Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The state in which persons are treated as equals in some respect.”
Equity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being fair and impartial, often involving adjustment to achieve fairness.”
Beneficence Beauchamp & Childress, "Principles of Biomedical Ethics" (2019) “The obligation to provide benefits and balance benefits against risks.”
Non-Maleficence Beauchamp & Childress, "Principles of Biomedical Ethics" (2019) “The obligation to avoid causing harm.”
Distributive Justice Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The morally proper distribution of social benefits and burdens.”
Retributive Justice Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The theory that punishment should be proportional to the crime committed.”
Corrective Justice Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The principle that wrongs should be rectified by compensation or punishment.”
Procedural Justice Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The fairness of the processes that lead to outcomes.”
Consent Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “Agreement to something, especially after thoughtful consideration.”
Informed Consent Beauchamp & Childress, "Principles of Biomedical Ethics" (2019) “An individual’s autonomous authorization of a medical intervention or participation in research.”
Confidentiality Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “The principle that information given in confidence should not be disclosed without permission.”
Fidelity Beauchamp & Childress, "Principles of Biomedical Ethics" (2019) “The duty to keep promises and commitments.”
Veracity Beauchamp & Childress, "Principles of Biomedical Ethics" (2019) “The obligation to tell the truth and not to lie or deceive others.”
Responsibility Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The state of being accountable for one’s actions or omissions.”
Accountability World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The obligation of power-holders to answer for their actions to those affected.”
Trust APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Confidence in the reliability, truth, ability, or strength of someone or something.”
Reciprocity Oxford Dictionary of Sociology (2020) “A social norm of responding to a positive action with another positive action.”
Solidarity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Unity or agreement of feeling or action, especially among individuals with a common interest.”
Stewardship World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The careful and responsible management of something entrusted to one’s care.”
Sustainability UN Brundtland Report, "Our Common Future" (1987) “Development that meets the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs.”
Precautionary Principle UNESCO, "The Precautionary Principle" (2005) “When human activities may lead to morally unacceptable harm that is scientifically plausible but uncertain, actions shall be taken to avoid or diminish that harm.”
Intergenerational Justice Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Fairness in the distribution of resources and opportunities between present and future generations.”
Intrinsic Value Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Value that something has in itself, for its own sake.”
Instrumental Value Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Value that something has as a means to an end.”
Extrinsic Value Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Value that depends on external relations or context.”
Final Value Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Value that is good in itself and not merely as a means.”
Conditional Value Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Value that holds only under certain conditions.”
Objective Value Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Value that exists independently of anyone’s desires or beliefs.”
Subjective Value Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Value that depends on the preferences or attitudes of valuers.”
Hedonism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that pleasure is the only intrinsic good and pain the only intrinsic evil.”
Well-Being Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The state of being healthy, happy, and prosperous.”
Eudaimonia Aristotle, "Nicomachean Ethics" (trans. Oxford, 2016) “The activity of the soul in accordance with virtue.”
Happiness APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A state of well-being characterized by emotions ranging from contentment to intense joy.”
Pleasure APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A feeling of satisfaction or enjoyment.”
Suffering APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The experience of pain or distress, either physical or psychological.”
Harm Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A setback to someone’s interests.”
Benefit Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “An advancement of someone’s interests.”
Risk Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “A situation involving exposure to danger.”
Uncertainty Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The state of being uncertain.”
Vulnerability APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The quality or state of being exposed to the possibility of being attacked or harmed.”
Resilience APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The ability to adapt to stress and adversity.”
Empowerment World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The process of increasing the capacity of individuals or groups to make choices and transform those choices into desired actions and outcomes.”
Exploitation Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The unfair use of another person’s labor or resources.”
Oppression Oxford Dictionary of Sociology (2020) “The exercise of authority or power in a burdensome, cruel, or unjust manner.”
Discrimination Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “The practice of treating a person or group differently from others in a way that is unfair.”
Prejudice APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A preconceived opinion that is not based on reason or actual experience.”
Stereotyping APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A fixed, overgeneralized belief about a particular group or class of people.”
Inclusion UNDP, "Human Development Report" (2020) “The action or state of including or being included within a group or structure.”
Diversity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The state of being diverse; variety.”
Tolerance Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The ability or willingness to tolerate the existence of opinions or behaviour that one dislikes or disagrees with.”
Respect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A feeling of deep admiration for someone or something elicited by their abilities, qualities, or achievements.”
Dignity UN Universal Declaration of Human Rights (1948) “The right of a person to be valued and respected for their own sake.”
Honor Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “High respect; great esteem.”
Integrity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being honest and having strong moral principles.”
4. VIRTUES & CHARACTER
Virtue Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A trait of character that is good for a person to have.”
Courage APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The ability to confront fear, pain, danger, uncertainty, or intimidation.”
Temperance Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Moderation or self-restraint, especially in the indulgence of appetites or passions.”
Prudence Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The virtue of practical wisdom, enabling one to judge correctly what is right and wrong in conduct.”
Justice Oxford Dictionary of Philosophy (2016) “The constant and perpetual disposition to render every man his due.”
Honesty Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being honest; truthfulness.”
Humility APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A modest or low view of one’s own importance.”
Compassion APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Sympathetic consciousness of others’ distress together with a desire to alleviate it.”
Empathy APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The ability to understand and share the feelings of another.”
Forgiveness APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The intentional and voluntary process by which a victim undergoes a change in feelings and attitude regarding an offense.”
Gratitude APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The state of being grateful; thankfulness.”
Patience Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The capacity to accept or tolerate delay, problems, or suffering without becoming annoyed or anxious.”
Resilience APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The ability to adapt to stress and adversity.”
Self-Discipline APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The ability to control one’s feelings and overcome one’s weaknesses.”
Loyalty Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “A strong feeling of support or allegiance.”
Wisdom APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The ability to make sound judgments and decisions based on experience and knowledge.”
Kindness Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being friendly, generous, and considerate.”
Generosity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being kind and generous.”
Fairness Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Impartial and just treatment or behaviour without favouritism or discrimination.”
Reliability Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being trustworthy or of performing consistently well.”
Trustworthiness Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The ability to be relied on as honest or truthful.”
Open-Mindedness APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Willingness to consider new ideas; unbias.”
Curiosity APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A strong desire to know or learn something.”
Creativity APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The use of imagination or original ideas to create something; inventiveness.”
Perseverance APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Persistence in doing something despite difficulty or delay in achieving success.”
Determination Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Firmness of purpose; resoluteness.”
Adaptability APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The quality of being able to adjust to new conditions.”
Flexibility APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The quality of bending easily without breaking; willingness to change or compromise.”
Optimism APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Hopefulness and confidence about the future or the success of something.”
Hope APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A feeling of expectation and desire for a particular thing to happen.”
Faith APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Complete trust or confidence in someone or something.”
Love APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “An intense feeling of deep affection.”
Joy APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A feeling of great pleasure and happiness.”
Peace Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Freedom from disturbance; tranquillity.”
Contentment Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “A state of happiness and satisfaction.”
Mindfulness APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The quality or state of being conscious or aware of something, especially in the present moment.”
Self-Awareness APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Conscious knowledge of one’s own character, feelings, motives, and desires.”
Self-Reflection APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Serious thought or consideration about one’s own experiences, actions, or motives.”
Self-Control APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The ability to control oneself, in particular one’s emotions and desires.”
Self-Confidence APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A feeling of trust in one’s abilities, qualities, and judgment.”
Self-Esteem APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Confidence in one’s own worth or abilities; self-respect.”
Self-Compassion APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Extending compassion to one’s self in instances of perceived inadequacy, failure, or general suffering.”
Authenticity APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The degree to which one is true to one’s own personality, spirit, or character.”
Sincerity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) "The quality of being free from pretense, deceit, or hypocrisy."
Candor Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being open and honest in expression; frankness.”
Transparency UNDP, "Human Development Report" (2020) “Openness in the functioning of institutions and access to information.”
Accountability World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The obligation of power-holders to answer for their actions to those affected.”
Reliability Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being trustworthy or of performing consistently well.”
Punctuality Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The fact of happening or doing something at the agreed or proper time.”
Diligence Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Careful and persistent work or effort.”
Thoroughness Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being complete or exhaustive.”
Precision Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being exact and accurate.”
Accuracy Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality or state of being correct or precise.”
Clarity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being coherent and intelligible.”
Simplicity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being easy to understand or do.”
Elegance Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being graceful and stylish in appearance or manner.”
Discretion Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of behaving or speaking in such a way as to avoid causing offense or revealing confidential information.”
Tact Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Skill and sensitivity in dealing with others or with difficult issues.”
5. PSYCHOLOGICAL CONSTRUCTS & EFFECTS
Dunning-Kruger Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A cognitive bias in which people with limited knowledge or competence in a domain overestimate their own ability.”
Placebo Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Improvement in a patient’s condition due to the patient’s belief in the efficacy of a treatment that has no therapeutic value.”
Nocebo Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A negative outcome or worsening of symptoms resulting from a patient’s expectation of harm from a treatment.”
Yerkes-Dodson Law APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The principle that performance increases with arousal up to an optimal point, beyond which further arousal impairs performance.”
Maslow's Hierarchy of Needs APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A motivational theory comprising a five-tier model of human needs, often depicted as hierarchical levels within a pyramid.”
Parkinson's Law Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The adage that work expands so as to fill the time available for its completion.”
Boomerang Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A persuasive message that has the opposite effect of that intended, causing the audience to strengthen their original attitude.”
Halo Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A cognitive bias in which an observer’s overall impression of a person influences their feelings and thoughts about that person’s character.”
Recency Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to remember the last items in a list better than those in the middle.”
Primacy Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to remember the first items in a list better than those in the middle.”
Framing Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A cognitive bias where people decide on options based on whether the options are presented with positive or negative connotations.”
Anchoring Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to rely too heavily on the first piece of information offered when making decisions.”
Availability Heuristic APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A mental shortcut that relies on immediate examples that come to a given person’s mind when evaluating a topic.”
Confirmation Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to search for, interpret, favor, and recall information in a way that confirms one’s preexisting beliefs.”
Cognitive Dissonance APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The mental discomfort experienced by a person who simultaneously holds two or more contradictory beliefs, values, or ideas.”
Fundamental Attribution Error APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to overemphasize personal characteristics and ignore situational factors in judging others’ behavior.”
Self-Serving Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to attribute positive outcomes to one’s own character but attribute negative outcomes to external factors.”
Groupthink APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A psychological phenomenon that occurs within a group of people in which the desire for harmony results in an irrational decision-making outcome.”
Bystander Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The phenomenon in which the greater the number of people present, the less likely people are to help a person in distress.”
Social Loafing APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency for individuals to expend less effort when working in a group than when working alone.”
Diffusion of Responsibility APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A sociopsychological phenomenon whereby a person is less likely to take responsibility for action when others are present.”
Moral Disengagement APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A process by which individuals convince themselves that ethical standards do not apply to them in a particular context.”
Reactance APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A motivational reaction to offers, persons, rules, or regulations that threaten or eliminate specific behavioral freedoms.”
Foot-in-the-Door APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A compliance technique in which a small request is followed by a larger request.”
Door-in-the-Face APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A compliance technique in which a large request is followed by a smaller, more reasonable request.”
Low-Ball Technique APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A compliance technique in which agreement is secured for an attractive offer, which is then made less attractive.”
That's-Not-All APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A compliance technique in which additional incentives are offered before the target responds to the initial request.”
Scarcity Principle APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to value rare or scarce objects more highly than abundant ones.”
Social Proof APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A psychological phenomenon where people assume the actions of others reflect correct behavior for a given situation.”
Authority Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to accept the opinions or commands of authority figures without sufficient critical evaluation.”
Liking APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A principle of influence whereby people are more likely to comply with requests from those they like.”
Reciprocity APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A social norm of responding to a positive action with another positive action.”
Commitment and Consistency APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to be consistent with what one has already done or said.”
Loss Aversion APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to prefer avoiding losses to acquiring equivalent gains.”
Sunk Cost Fallacy APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to continue an endeavor once an investment in money, effort, or time has been made.”
Status Quo Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A preference for the current state of affairs.”
Endowment Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency for people to demand much more to give up an object than they would be willing to pay to acquire it.”
Prospect Theory APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A behavioral model that shows how people decide between alternatives that involve risk and uncertainty.”
Mental Accounting APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to categorize and treat money differently depending on where it comes from or where it is going.”
Overconfidence Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Excessive confidence in one’s own answers to questions.”
Hindsight Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The inclination, after an event has occurred, to see the event as having been predictable.”
Illusion of Control APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to overestimate one’s degree of influence over external events.”
Gambler’s Fallacy APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The mistaken belief that if something happens more frequently than normal during a given period, it will happen less frequently in the future.”
Hot Hand Fallacy APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The belief that a person who has experienced success with a random event has a greater chance of further success in additional attempts.”
Conjunction Fallacy APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to believe that specific conditions are more probable than a single general one.”
Base Rate Neglect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to ignore general information and focus on information pertaining to the specific case.”
Representativeness Heuristic APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A mental shortcut that involves judging the probability of an event by how closely it resembles a prototype.”
Affect Heuristic APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A mental shortcut that relies on current emotions to make decisions.”
Planning Fallacy APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to underestimate the time needed to complete a future task.”
Optimism Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to be over-optimistic about the outcome of planned actions.”
Pessimism Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to overestimate the likelihood of negative outcomes.”
Negativity Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to give more attention and weight to negative information than positive information.”
Positivity Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to remember positive experiences more than negative ones.”
Peak-End Rule APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to judge an experience based on its most intense point and its end, rather than the average.”
Duration Neglect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to ignore the duration of an experience when evaluating it.”
Serial Position Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to recall the first and last items in a list better than the middle items.”
Zeigarnik Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to remember incomplete or interrupted tasks better than completed tasks.”
Von Restorff Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to remember distinctive or novel items better than common ones.”
Spacing Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The phenomenon whereby learning is better when study sessions are spaced out over time.”
Testing Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The finding that retrieving information from memory improves long-term retention.”
Interleaving APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A learning technique that involves mixing different topics or forms of practice to improve learning.”
Self-Reference Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to better remember information that is related to oneself.”
Generation Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The phenomenon that information is better remembered if it is generated from one’s own mind rather than simply read.”
Mere Exposure Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to develop a preference for things merely because they are familiar.”
Illusory Truth Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to believe false information to be correct after repeated exposure.”
False Consensus Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to overestimate the degree to which others agree with one’s own beliefs.”
Third-Person Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The hypothesis that people tend to perceive that mass media messages have a greater effect on others than on themselves.”
Barnum Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to accept vague or general personality descriptions as uniquely applicable to oneself.”
Spotlight Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to overestimate how much others notice and care about one’s appearance or behavior.”
Illusion of Transparency APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to overestimate the degree to which others can discern one’s internal states.”
Curse of Knowledge APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The cognitive bias of assuming that others have the same background knowledge to understand.”
Empathy Gap APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to underestimate the influence of varying mental states on behavior.”
Projection Bias APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency to assume that others share one’s current emotions, thoughts, or values.”
False Memory APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A memory of an event that did not happen or a distortion of an event that did occur.”
Source Amnesia APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The inability to remember the source of a memory while retaining the factual content.”
Misinformation Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency for post-event information to interfere with the memory of the original event.”
Suggestibility APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The quality of being inclined to accept and act on the suggestions of others.”
Compliance APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A change in behavior in response to a direct request.”
Obedience APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Compliance with an order, request, or law or submission to another’s authority.”
Conformity APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A change in behavior or belief to accord with others.”
Normative Influence APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Conformity based on the desire to be liked or accepted by others.”
Informational Influence APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Conformity based on the desire to be correct and to have accurate perceptions.”
Deindividuation APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A state of reduced self-awareness and lowered concern for social evaluation, often occurring in group settings.”
Social Facilitation APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency for the presence of others to enhance performance on simple or well-learned tasks.”
Social Inhibition APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency for the presence of others to impair performance on complex or unfamiliar tasks.”
Audience Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The impact of a passive audience on individual performance.”
Co-Action Effect APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The enhancement of performance when working alongside others on the same task.”
Risk Shift APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency for groups to make riskier decisions than individuals.”
Caution Shift APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency for groups to make more cautious decisions than individuals.”
Polarization APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The tendency for group discussion to intensify the average predisposition of group members.”
Pluralistic Ignorance APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A situation in which a majority of group members privately reject a norm, but incorrectly assume that most others accept it.”
Social Trap APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A situation in which conflicting parties pursue their self-interest and end up worse off than if they had cooperated.”
Tragedy of the Commons APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A situation in which individuals acting independently deplete a shared resource, to the detriment of all.”
Prisoner's Dilemma Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A game-theoretic scenario in which two rational agents, acting in their own self-interest, fail to cooperate even when cooperation would benefit both.”
Ultimatum Game APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A game in which one player proposes how to divide a resource, and the other can accept or reject the proposal.”
Dictator Game APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A game in which one player unilaterally determines the division of a resource between themselves and another player.”
Public Goods Game APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A game in which participants decide how much to contribute to a common pool that benefits all.”
Trust Game APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A game designed to measure trust and reciprocity between two players.”
Altruistic Punishment APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The act of punishing norm violators at a personal cost to enforce cooperation.”
Reputation APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The beliefs or opinions that are generally held about someone or something.”
Gossip APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Informal conversation or reports about other people, typically involving details not confirmed as true.”
Indirect Reciprocity APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Cooperation based on reputation: helping those who have helped others.”
Costly Signaling APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “Behaviors that are costly to perform but signal underlying qualities like strength or generosity.”
Virtue Signaling Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The action or practice of publicly expressing opinions or sentiments intended to demonstrate one’s good character or moral correctness.”
6. PUBLIC SERVICE & GOVERNANCE
Public Interest Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “The welfare of the general public (in contrast to the selfish interest of a person, group, or firm) in which the whole society has a stake.”
Good Governance World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The traditions and institutions by which authority in a country is exercised for the common good.”
Transparency UNDP, "Human Development Report" (2020) “Openness in the functioning of institutions and access to information.”
Accountability World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The obligation of power-holders to answer for their actions to those affected.”
Impartiality Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Equal treatment of all rivals or disputants; fairness.”
Objectivity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Judgment based on observable phenomena and uninfluenced by emotions or personal prejudices.”
Integrity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of being honest and having strong moral principles.”
Probity Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The quality of having strong moral principles; honesty and decency.”
Conflict of Interest Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “A situation in which a person is in a position to derive personal benefit from actions or decisions made in their official capacity.”
Corruption Transparency International, "Global Corruption Report" (2020) “The abuse of entrusted power for private gain.”
Nepotism Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The practice among those with power or influence of favouring relatives or friends, especially by giving them jobs.”
Cronyism Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The appointment of friends and associates to positions of authority, without proper regard to their qualifications.”
Bribery Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “The offering, giving, receiving, or soliciting of something of value for the purpose of influencing the action of an official in the discharge of public duties.”
Embezzlement Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “The fraudulent appropriation of property by a person to whom it has been entrusted.”
Fraud Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “Wrongful or criminal deception intended to result in financial or personal gain.”
Whistleblowing Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The disclosure by an employee or other insider of illegal, immoral, or illegitimate practices under the control of their employers, to persons or organizations that may be able to effect action.”
Rule of Law World Justice Project, "Rule of Law Index" (2020) “A system in which the laws are clear, publicized, stable, and just; are applied evenly; and protect fundamental rights.”
Due Process Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “The administration of justice according to established rules and principles, ensuring fairness and fundamental justice.”
Equity Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “A system of justice that supplements the common law by providing remedies where the strict rules of law would cause injustice.”
Access to Justice UNDP, "Human Development Report" (2020) “The ability of people to seek and obtain a remedy through formal or informal institutions of justice.”
Participation UNDP, "Human Development Report" (2020) “The involvement of citizens in decision-making processes that affect their lives.”
Inclusiveness UNDP, "Human Development Report" (2020) “Ensuring that all groups, especially the marginalized, are represented and have a voice in governance.”
Responsiveness World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The extent to which policies and institutions respond to the needs and preferences of citizens.”
Effectiveness World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The capacity of government to achieve intended outcomes.”
Efficiency World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The optimal use of resources to achieve desired outcomes.”
Consensus Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “General agreement.”
Legitimacy Oxford Dictionary of Politics (2020) “The acceptance of the authority of a government or institution by the public.”
Public Trust World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The confidence that citizens have in the reliability and integrity of public institutions.”
Social Contract Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “An implicit agreement among the members of a society to cooperate for social benefits.”
Civil Service Oxford Dictionary of Politics (2020) “The permanent professional branches of a government’s administration, excluding military and judicial branches and elected politicians.”
Meritocracy Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Government or the holding of power by people selected according to merit.”
Bureaucracy Oxford Dictionary of Politics (2020) “A system of government in which most of the important decisions are taken by state officials rather than elected representatives.”
Red Tape Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “Excessive bureaucracy or adherence to rules and formalities, especially in public business.”
Policy Oxford Dictionary of Politics (2020) “A course or principle of action adopted or proposed by an organization or individual.”
Regulation Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “A rule or directive made and maintained by an authority.”
Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “Government intervention in markets to control or influence economic behavior.”
Compliance Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “The action or fact of complying with a wish or command.”
Enforcement Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “The act of compelling observance of or compliance with a law, rule, or obligation.”
Sanction Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “A penalty for disobeying a law or rule.”
Incentive Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “A payment or concession to stimulate greater output or investment.”
Subsidy Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “A sum of money granted by the state to assist an industry or business so that the price of a commodity or service may remain low.”
Tax Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “A compulsory contribution to state revenue, levied by the government on workers’ income and business profits, or added to the cost of some goods, services, and transactions.”
Budget Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “A financial plan for a defined period, often one year, showing expected revenues and expenditures.”
Deficit Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “The amount by which spending exceeds revenue over a particular period.”
Debt Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “The total amount of money owed by a government or individual.”
Inflation Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “A general increase in prices and fall in the purchasing value of money.”
Recession Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “A period of temporary economic decline during which trade and industrial activity are reduced.”
Unemployment Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “The state of being without a job while actively seeking work.”
Poverty World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The condition of having insufficient resources or income to meet basic needs.”
Inequality World Bank, "World Development Report" (2020) “The unequal distribution of income, wealth, or opportunities among individuals or groups.”
Social Welfare Oxford Dictionary of Sociology (2020) “Organized provision of resources and services to meet the needs of the disadvantaged.”
Public Goods Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “Goods that are non-excludable and non-rivalrous, such as national defense or clean air.”
Externalities Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “Costs or benefits that affect a party who did not choose to incur that cost or benefit.”
Market Failure Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “A situation in which the allocation of goods and services by a free market is not efficient.”
Government Failure Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “A situation where government intervention in the economy leads to an inefficient allocation of resources.”
Privatization Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “The transfer of ownership of property or businesses from a government to a privately owned entity.”
Nationalization Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “The transfer of a major branch of industry or commerce from private to state ownership or control.”
Deregulation Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “The removal or simplification of government rules and regulations that constrain the operation of market forces.”
7. APPLIED ETHICS DOMAINS
Bioethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of the ethical issues emerging from advances in biology and medicine.”
Medical Ethics Beauchamp & Childress, "Principles of Biomedical Ethics" (2019) “The disciplined study of morality in medicine.”
Euthanasia Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The painless killing of a patient suffering from an incurable and painful disease or in an irreversible coma.”
Assisted Suicide Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The intentional act of providing a person with the medical means to end their own life.”
Abortion Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The deliberate termination of a human pregnancy.”
Stem Cell Research Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Research involving the use of stem cells, especially embryonic stem cells, to treat disease or injury.”
Genetic Engineering Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The deliberate modification of the characteristics of an organism by manipulating its genetic material.”
CRISPR Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A family of DNA sequences used in genome editing to make precise changes to genes.”
Designer Babies Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Infants whose genetic characteristics have been artificially selected by genetic engineering.”
Cloning Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The process of producing genetically identical individuals of an organism.”
Human Enhancement Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The use of technological means to select or alter human characteristics beyond what is necessary to sustain health.”
Transhumanism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A philosophical and cultural movement that advocates the use of technology to enhance human physical and cognitive abilities.”
Neuroethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of the ethical, legal, and social implications of neuroscience.”
Brain-Computer Interface Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A direct communication pathway between the brain and an external device.”
Informed Consent Beauchamp & Childress, "Principles of Biomedical Ethics" (2019) “An individual’s autonomous authorization of a medical intervention or participation in research.”
Placebo APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A substance or treatment with no active therapeutic effect.”
Clinical Trial World Medical Association, "Declaration of Helsinki" (2013) “A research study involving human subjects designed to answer specific questions about biomedical interventions.”
Randomized Controlled Trial Oxford Dictionary of Statistics (2020) “An experiment in which participants are randomly allocated to receive one of several clinical interventions.”
Double-Blind Study APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “A study in which neither the participants nor the experimenters know who is receiving a particular treatment.”
Therapeutic Misconception Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The tendency of research participants to mistakenly believe that the primary purpose of a clinical trial is to provide therapeutic benefit.”
Organ Donation Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The process of surgically removing an organ from one person and placing it into another.”
Opt-In System Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A system requiring explicit consent for organ donation after death.”
Opt-Out System Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A system in which individuals are presumed to consent to organ donation unless they explicitly opt out.”
Xenotransplantation Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The transplantation of living cells, tissues, or organs from one species to another.”
Resource Allocation Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The distribution of scarce medical resources among competing needs.”
Triage Oxford Dictionary of Nursing (2020) “The process of determining the priority of patients’ treatments based on the severity of their condition.”
QALY Oxford Dictionary of Public Health (2020) “Quality-Adjusted Life Year: a measure of disease burden, including both the quality and quantity of life lived.”
DALY World Health Organization, "Global Burden of Disease" (2020) “Disability-Adjusted Life Year: a measure of overall disease burden, expressed as the number of years lost due to ill-health, disability, or early death.”
Cost-Effectiveness Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “The extent to which a policy or intervention provides benefits relative to its costs.”
Pandemic Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The application of ethical principles to public health crises involving widespread infectious disease.”
Vaccine Mandates Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Government requirements that individuals receive certain vaccinations.”
Lockdown Oxford Dictionary of Public Health (2020) “A protocol requiring people to stay where they are, usually due to specific risks to themselves or others.”
Contact Tracing WHO, "Contact Tracing for COVID-19" (2020) “The process of identifying, assessing, and managing people who have been exposed to a disease to prevent onward transmission.”
Reproductive Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of ethical issues arising from human reproduction and reproductive technologies.”
IVF Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “In Vitro Fertilization: the process of fertilization by extracting eggs, retrieving a sperm sample, and then manually combining them in a laboratory dish.”
Surrogacy Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “An arrangement in which a woman carries and delivers a child for another couple or person.”
Commercial Surrogacy Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A surrogacy arrangement in which the surrogate mother is compensated beyond medical and living expenses.”
Altruistic Surrogacy Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A surrogacy arrangement in which the surrogate receives no financial compensation.”
Embryo Adoption Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The transfer of frozen embryos from one couple to another for implantation and gestation.”
Fetal Rights Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The moral or legal entitlements attributed to a fetus.”
Maternal-Fetal Conflict Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A situation in which the interests of a pregnant woman and her fetus come into conflict.”
Environmental Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The discipline in philosophy that studies the moral relationship of human beings to the environment.”
Anthropocentrism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that human beings are the central or most significant entities in the world.”
Biocentrism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The ethical perspective that all living organisms have inherent value.”
Ecocentrism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The view that ecosystems as a whole have moral value.”
Deep Ecology Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A philosophy that calls for a profound shift in human relationships with the natural world.”
Land Ethic Aldo Leopold, "A Sand County Almanac" (1949) “A thing is right when it tends to preserve the integrity, stability, and beauty of the biotic community. It is wrong when it tends otherwise.”
Animal Rights Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The idea that some, or all, nonhuman animals are entitled to possession of their own lives.”
Animal Welfare Oxford Dictionary of Zoology (2020) “The physical and mental well-being of animals, especially in captivity.”
Speciesism Peter Singer, "Animal Liberation" (1975) “A prejudice or attitude of bias in favor of the interests of members of one’s own species and against those of members of other species.”
Veganism Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The practice of eating only food not derived from animals and typically also of avoiding the use of animal products.”
Vegetarianism Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “The practice of not eating meat or fish.”
Factory Farming Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A system of rearing livestock using intensive methods to maximize production.”
Climate Change Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of the moral dimensions of human-induced climate change.”
Carbon Footprint Oxford Dictionary of Environment and Conservation (2020) “The total amount of greenhouse gases produced to directly and indirectly support human activities, usually expressed in equivalent tons of carbon dioxide.”
Carbon Offset Oxford Dictionary of Environment and Conservation (2020) “A reduction in emissions of carbon dioxide or other greenhouse gases made in order to compensate for emissions made elsewhere.”
Geoengineering Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Deliberate large-scale intervention in the Earth’s natural systems to counteract climate change.”
Solar Radiation Management Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A set of proposed methods to reduce global warming by reflecting sunlight away from Earth.”
Carbon Capture Oxford Dictionary of Environment and Conservation (2020) “The process of trapping carbon dioxide at its emission source and storing it to avoid release into the atmosphere.”
Business Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of moral issues that arise in the context of commerce.”

Shareholder Primacy Milton Friedman, "The Social Responsibility of Business Is to Increase Its Profits" (1970) “The only social responsibility of business is to increase its profits.”
Insider Trading Oxford Dictionary of Finance and Banking (2020) “The illegal practice of trading on the stock exchange to one’s own advantage through having access to confidential information.”
Whistleblowing Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The disclosure by an employee or other insider of illegal, immoral, or illegitimate practices under the control of their employers.”

Price Gouging Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Charging excessively high prices for necessities during an emergency.”
Monopoly Oxford Dictionary of Economics (2020) “A market structure characterized by a single seller, selling a unique product in the market.”

Intellectual Property Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “Intangible creations of the human intellect, protected by law.”

Copyright Oxford Dictionary of Law (2020) “The exclusive legal right to reproduce, publish, sell, or distribute the matter and form of something.”

Data Privacy Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The right of individuals to control access to their personal information.”
GDPR EU Regulation 2016/679 (2018) “General Data Protection Regulation: a regulation on data protection and privacy in the European Union.”
Surveillance Capitalism Shoshana Zuboff, "The Age of Surveillance Capitalism" (2019) “A new economic order that claims human experience as free raw material for hidden commercial practices of extraction, prediction, and sales.”
Algorithmic Bias Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Systematic and unfair discrimination resulting from algorithmic decision-making.”
Digital Divide UNDP, "Human Development Report" (2020) “The gap between those who have access to modern information and communication technology and those who do not.”
Cyberethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of moral issues involving cyber technology.”
Hacking Oxford Dictionary of Computing (2020) “Gaining unauthorized access to data in a system or computer.”
Cyberbullying APA Dictionary of Psychology (2020) “The use of electronic communication to bully a person, typically by sending messages of an intimidating or threatening nature.”
Deepfake Oxford Dictionary of English (2020) “A video of a person in which their face or body has been digitally altered so that they appear to be someone else.”
AI Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The branch of ethics that studies moral issues arising from the design, development, and deployment of artificial intelligence.”
Machine Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The field concerned with ensuring that autonomous systems behave ethically.”
Superintelligence Nick Bostrom, "Superintelligence" (2014) “Any intellect that greatly exceeds the cognitive performance of humans in virtually all domains of interest.”
Existential Risk Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A risk that threatens the destruction of humanity’s long-term potential.”
Autonomous Weapons Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Weapons systems that can select and engage targets without human intervention.”
War Ethics Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The study of moral issues in armed conflict.”
Just War Theory Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “A doctrine of military ethics that aims to ensure war is morally justifiable.”

Civil Disobedience Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The active, professed refusal to obey certain laws, demands, or commands of a government.”
Terrorism Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The use of violence against non-combatants for political, religious, or ideological ends.”
Torture Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “The intentional infliction of severe pain or suffering, whether physical or mental, upon a person.”
Human Rights UN Universal Declaration of Human Rights (1948) “Rights inherent to all human beings, regardless of race, sex, nationality, ethnicity, language, religion, or any other status.”
Genocide UN Convention on Genocide (1948) “Acts committed with intent to destroy, in whole or in part, a national, ethnical, racial or religious group.”

Sweatshop Regulation Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy (2021) “Efforts to improve working conditions in low-wage manufacturing through legal or voluntary standards.”
- All Indian constitutional articles, laws, acts, policies, etc for ethics -
Key Events/Initiatives Type Details (Ethical Focus & Key Provisions)
PRE-INDEPENDENCE (1800s–1947)
Indian Penal Code, 1860 Act Description:
  • Introduced by Lord Macaulay under British India
  • First comprehensive criminal code in India
  • Sections 161–165 (later repealed) specifically penalized public servants
  • Penalized accepting bribes or gratuities
  • Established criminal liability for abuse of official position
Ethical focus:
  • Integrity in public office
  • Prevention of corruption
  • Accountability of colonial administrators
  • Foundation of public servant liability
Indian Evidence Act, 1872 Act Description:
  • Regulates admissibility of evidence in Indian courts
  • Section 123: Protects official communications from disclosure
  • Section 114: Allows courts to presume corruption in certain cases
  • Ensures structured use of evidence in corruption trials
Ethical focus:
  • Fairness in judicial proceedings
  • Veracity and reliability of evidence
  • Ethical use of official information
  • Judicial integrity
Code of Criminal Procedure, 1898 Act Description:
  • Governs arrest, investigation, and trial procedures
  • Sections 154–176: Mandates FIR registration
  • Requires fair and impartial investigation
  • Protects against arbitrary police action
Ethical focus:
  • Due process of law
  • Accountability in criminal investigations
  • Protection from arbitrary arrest
  • Ethical policing standards
Prevention of Corruption Act Roots (Colonial Era) Act (Legacy) Description:
  • Early anti-corruption provisions under IPC and Police Act, 1861
  • Public servants liable for criminal misconduct
  • Laid foundation for independent anti-corruption law
  • Precursor to Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988
Ethical focus:
  • Public servant integrity
  • Deterrence against misuse of power
  • Accountability in colonial bureaucracy
POST-INDEPENDENCE (1947–1970s)
Articles 14 & 21 (1950) Constitutional Article Description:
  • Article 14: Equality before the law and equal protection
  • Article 21: No deprivation of life/liberty except by procedure established by law
  • Expanded in Maneka Gandhi case (1978) to include fairness and reasonableness
Ethical focus:
  • Non-arbitrary governance
  • Integrity in public actions
  • Protection from unethical state overreach
  • Rule of law as ethical foundation
Article 48A & 51A(g) (1976, 42nd Amendment) Constitutional Article Description:
  • Article 48A: State duty to protect and improve the environment
  • Article 51A(g): Fundamental duty of citizens to protect nature, wildlife, forests
  • Introduced via 42nd Constitutional Amendment
Ethical focus:
  • Intergenerational equity
  • Moral responsibility for sustainability
  • Civic environmental ethics
  • State-citizen partnership in conservation
Indian Medical Council Act, 1956 Act Description:
  • Established Medical Council of India (MCI)
  • Regulates medical education and professional practice
  • Section 20A: Empowers code of ethics (2002 Regulations)
  • Prohibits fee splitting, false advertising, unethical practices
Ethical focus:
  • Professional integrity
  • Patient confidentiality
  • Trust in doctor-patient relationship
  • Ethical medical conduct
All India Service (Conduct) Rules, 1968 Rule Description:
  • Applies to IAS, IPS, Indian Forest Service
  • Rule 3: Maintain absolute integrity and devotion to duty
  • Rule 16: Annual immovable property return mandatory
  • Rule 4: No political activity or membership
Ethical focus:
  • Political neutrality
  • Conflict of interest avoidance
  • Transparency in assets
  • Ethical civil service conduct
Medical Termination of Pregnancy Act, 1971 (Amended 2021) Act Description:
  • Allows termination up to 20 weeks (24 weeks in special cases)
  • Requires one/two doctor opinions based on gestational age
  • Mandatory consent of the woman
  • Amended in 2021 to expand access and privacy
Ethical focus:
  • Women’s bodily autonomy
  • Medical ethics in reproductive care
  • Beneficence and non-maleficence
  • Confidentiality and dignity
Nolan Committee Principles (1995, UK; Adopted via 2nd ARC) International Guideline Description:
  • 7 principles: Selflessness, Integrity, Objectivity, Accountability, Openness, Honesty, Leadership
  • Recommended by 2nd ARC (2007) for Indian civil service code
  • Adopted as ethical benchmark for public servants
Ethical focus:
  • Universal ethical governance standards
  • Leadership by example
  • Moral accountability in public life
1980s–1990s
Benami Transactions (Prohibition) Act, 1988 Act Description:
  • Prohibits holding property in another’s name to evade tax or law
  • Section 3: Bans all benami transactions
  • Penalty: Up to 3 years imprisonment
  • Foundation for 2016 amendment
Ethical focus:
  • Financial transparency
  • Anti-corruption in asset holding
  • Prevention of black money
  • Tax justice
Prevention of Corruption Act, 1988 Act Description:
  • Consolidates and strengthens anti-corruption laws
  • Sections 7–12: Criminalize taking bribe, criminal misconduct
  • Defines “public servant” broadly
  • 2018 amendment protects bona fide decisions
Ethical focus:
  • Integrity in public service
  • Zero tolerance for undue advantage
  • Public trust in governance
Representation of the People Act, 1951 (Key Amendments 1988–1996) Act Description:
  • Section 8: Disqualification on conviction for certain offenses
  • Section 123: Defines corrupt practices (bribery, undue influence)
  • 1988 amendment introduced anti-defection law (10th Schedule)
Ethical focus:
  • Clean politics
  • Free and fair elections
  • Voter sovereignty
  • Electoral integrity
Model Code of Conduct (Election Commission of India, 1991) Policy Description:
  • Voluntary code enforced during elections

  • Bans voter inducements, personal attacks
  • Enforced via ECI observers and expenditure monitoring
Ethical focus:
  • Electoral integrity

  • Democratic fairness
Right to Information Act, 2005 (Roots in 1990s PILs & FOI Movement) Act Description:
  • Section 4: Proactive disclosure by public authorities
  • Section 8: Limited exemptions for national security
  • Overrides Official Secrets Act, 1923
  • Roots in 1990s freedom of information movement
Ethical focus:
  • Transparency in governance

Ethical focus:

  • Drafted in 1990s, enacted in some states post-2006
Ethical focus:

Ethical focus:

United Nations Declaration Universal Declaration of Human Rights (UDHR), 1948 Description:
  • Adopted by UN General Assembly on 10 Dec 1948
  • 30 articles on fundamental human rights
  • Proclaims inherent dignity and equality of all humans
  • Influences national constitutions and laws worldwide
Ethical focus:

Ethical focus:

PRE-INDEPENDENCE (1800s–1947)
Indian Medical Association (IMA), 1928 Voluntary Body Description:
  • Founded as All India Medical Association
  • Represents 3.5 lakh+ doctors across India
  • Voluntary body for medical ethics, education, and advocacy
  • Historically advocated ethical practice during colonial era
Ethical focus:
  • Professional integrity
  • Patient rights
  • Issues code of ethics
  • Promotes ethical medical conduct
Indian Council of Medical Research (ICMR) Precursor Bodies, 1911 Precursor Body Description:
  • Roots in Advisory Board for Medical Research
  • Established under British India for scientific inquiry
  • Apex biomedical research body precursor
  • Evolved into modern ICMR post-independence
Ethical focus:
  • Early ethics guidelines for human trials
  • Foundation for research integrity
  • Precursor to National Ethical Guidelines (2017)

  • Maintains Indian Medical Register
  • Enforces code of medical ethics
  • Dissolved in 2020, replaced by NMC
Ethical focus:
  • Professional misconduct inquiries
  • Patient confidentiality
  • Ethical standards in medical education

  • Chaired by Morarji Desai
  • 20 reports on administrative efficiency
  • Recommended Code of Ethics for public servants
  • Advocated anti-corruption measures
  • Suggested separation of judiciary
Ethical focus:

  • Chapter 10: Corruption Prevention
  • Vigilance & asset declaration
  • Precursor to All India Services (Conduct) Rules
Key quote: "Public servants should be accountable to the people, and the government should be accountable to Parliament." Source: 1st ARC Final Report, 1970.

  • Report No. 4
  • Recommended merit-based recruitment
  • Proposed ethical training modules
  • Suggested Ethics Committee for civil services
Ethical focus:
  • Accountability
  • Impartiality
  • Integrity in decision-making
Key quote: "The public servant must act with integrity and be accountable for his decisions." Source: 1st ARC, Report No. 4, p. 112.

  • Influenced Lokpal Act, RTI amendments
Ethical focus:
  • Systemic ethics
  • Institutional integrity

- Current affairs -
- Chapter 1 - ETHICS AND HUMAN INTERFACE - 1. DEFINING ETHICS - Oxford dictionary - Ethics is the branch of philosophy that studies moral principles — the concepts of right and wrong, good and bad, virtue and vice, justice, and moral responsibility. - Cambridge Dictionary: - Ethics refers to a system of accepted beliefs which control behaviour, especially such a system based on morals. - 2. SCOPE OF ETHICS - Ethics deals with human actions, not the actions of humans. Human action (Ethical Action) is deliberate and involves knowledge/consciousness, free will/choice and voluntariness/willingness, like reading, running, and smoking but all actions of humans (Non-Ethical Action) may not be deliberate, like sneezing, yawing, belches etc. - The province or scope of ethics is the range of its subject matter. Ethics, as a normative science, seeks to define moral ideals. It is not concerned with nature, origin or development of human conduct; but the ideal or standard to which our conduct should conform. But to lay down the ideal of conduct, it must know the nature of the conduct. - Conduct is expression of character. Character is settled habit of will. It is the permanent disposition produced by habitual actions. - Ethics is sometimes said to be science of character.
